Specialist AI: JAH Prescriptive AI Librarian (Signature)

JAH Prescriptive AI Librarian (Signature) answers questions about this database, explains any record in plain sentences, compares records side by side on the same fields, and helps a reader follow the records in order. It never presents a generated design as a public record, and it never presents a design as a working service.

Boundless generator

The generator is seeded and deterministic, throttled to at most 100 records per call, duplicate-checked by deterministic hash ids, and hard-stopped at 1,000,000,000 records. Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files. Every generated record is labeled src "generated". Open the page script to use window.PrescriptiveAiSignatureTools.

Validation engine

Each record is checked for a complete title, its own record kind, its own edition slug and id prefix, a full prose description, and a demo whose stored output byte-matches a fresh run.

Signature LP Solver: A Design for Linear Optimization JAH-PRAIS-000001 · 1

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature LP Solver is a Signature-made design for solving linear programs, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes a goal and linear constraints, walks the corners of the feasible region by the simplex method, and reports the optimum with the constraints that bind. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of linear programming from Kantorovich in 1939 and Dantzig's simplex method in 1947. The design takes the corner-walking idea, and adds the binding report: the solver names the constraints that hold the optimum in place, so the user can see what limits it. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature LP Solver is a Signature-made design for solving linear programs, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes a goal and linear constraints, walks the corners of the feasible region by the simplex method, and reports the optimum with the constraints that bind. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature LP Solver, a Signature-made design-study for linear programming, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the decision variables, the objective, and the constraints as linear inequalities. Step 2: find a feasible corner of the region the constraints define. Step 3: walk to a neighbouring corner that improves the objective, until no neighbour improves it. Step 4: report the optimum, the corner, and the constraints that bind. The demo runs a small worked example of the choice step with illustrative design values: it takes five production options with costs of 35, 50, 28, 65, and 45 and benefits of 105, 150, 90, 195, and 130, and reports the best option by benefit per cost, the total cost, and the total benefit. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"best_ratio_design": 3.2143, "total_cost_design": 223, "total_benefit_design": 670, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the solver walks the corners and reports the binding constraints, and the worked example shows the choice arithmetic. The benefit-per-cost ratios are 3.0, 3.0, 3.2143, 3.0, and 2.8889, so the best option is the third, with a ratio of 3.2143, and the totals are a cost of 223 and a benefit of 670. The result that matters is the design judgment: a solver that names its binding constraints can be argued with, and one that reports only the optimum cannot, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: best: option 3, best_ratio: 3.2143, total_benefit: 670, total_cost: 223.

When It Applies

The Signature LP Solver design applies as a study for anyone building an optimizer: it shows how to walk the corners and report the binding constraints. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the problem is nonlinear, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The solver is a design, not a system, so it has never solved a real linear program, and its values are illustrative. The design assumes the problem is linear, which real problems often are not, and it needs every constraint written down, which they often are not. The design is a study of how a linear solver should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a solver exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of ratios. The five options have benefit-per-cost ratios of 105 over 35, which is 3.0; 150 over 50, which is 3.0; 90 over 28, which is 3.2143; 195 over 65, which is 3.0; and 130 over 45, which is 2.8889. The best ratio is 3.2143, for the third option. The total cost is 35 plus 50 plus 28 plus 65 plus 45, which is 223, and the total benefit is 105 plus 150 plus 90 plus 195 plus 130, which is 670. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: score = benefit / cost for each option; best = the option with the largest score; total cost = sum of costs; total benefit = sum of benefits. The equation states the choice check the demo performs: it scores every option by benefit divided by cost, picks the largest score as the best option, and totals the costs and benefits.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of constrained optimization: a linear objective over a region defined by linear constraints always reaches its optimum at a corner, so the search can walk the corners systematically. The design follows the public evidence that the simplex method is transparent because it walks the corners, and it adds the binding report, so the transparency extends to the answer. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A prescriptive choice compares options by what each one returns for what it costs; the best option is the one with the largest benefit per unit of cost.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
options = [('option 1', 35, 105), ('option 2', 50, 150), ('option 3', 28, 90), ('option 4', 65, 195), ('option 5', 45, 130)]
def compute():
    scored = [(b / c, name) for name, c, b in options]
    best_ratio_raw, best = max(scored)
    best_ratio = round(best_ratio_raw, 4)
    total_cost = sum(c for _, c, _ in options)
    total_benefit = sum(b for _, _, b in options)
    out = {"best": best, "best_ratio": best_ratio, "total_cost": total_cost, "total_benefit": total_benefit}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class ProgJAHPRAIS000001:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        options = [('option 1', 35, 105), ('option 2', 50, 150), ('option 3', 28, 90), ('option 4', 65, 195), ('option 5', 45, 130)]
        def compute():
            scored = [(b / c, name) for name, c, b in options]
            best_ratio_raw, best = max(scored)
            best_ratio = round(best_ratio_raw, 4)
            total_cost = sum(c for _, c, _ in options)
            total_benefit = sum(b for _, _, b in options)
            out = {"best": best, "best_ratio": best_ratio, "total_cost": total_cost, "total_benefit": total_benefit}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHPRAIS000001().run())

Signature OR Workbench: A Design for Modeling Decisions JAH-PRAIS-000002 · 2

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature OR Workbench is a Signature-made design for modeling a decision problem, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design gives the analyst a bench for stating the problem, building the model, solving it, and testing the solution against the real world, with the assumptions written down at each step. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of operations research from Blackett's Circus in 1940 and the Operational Research Society in 1948. The design takes the discipline the public record shows, that a decision is a thing that can be studied, and makes the assumptions part of the bench: every model carries its assumptions on its face, so the solution can be judged with them in view. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature OR Workbench is a Signature-made design for modeling a decision problem, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design gives the analyst a bench for stating the problem, building the model, solving it, and testing the solution against the real world, with the assumptions written down at each step. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature OR Workbench, a Signature-made design-study for operations research, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the decision problem, the objective, and the constraints. Step 2: build a model of the problem, with the variables, the objective, and the constraints written down. Step 3: solve the model, by optimization, simulation, or analysis. Step 4: test the solution against the real world, and act on it if it survives the test. The demo runs a small worked example of the counting step with illustrative design values: it takes five analyses with cases studied of 15, 22, 11, 28, and 19, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"total_cases_design": 95, "mean_cases_design": 19.0, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the workbench states, models, solves, and tests, and the worked example shows the counting arithmetic. The five analyses study 15, 22, 11, 28, and 19 cases, for a total of 95, a mean of 19.0, a smallest of 11, and a largest of 28. The result that matters is the design judgment: a bench that writes its assumptions on the model's face can be judged, and one that hides them cannot, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. When It Applies

It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the problem cannot be stated, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The workbench is a design, not a system, so it has never modeled a real decision, and its counts are illustrative. The design assumes the problem can be stated, which real problems often cannot be, and a model leaves things out, and the things left out can matter more than the things included. The design is a study of how decisions should be modeled, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a workbench exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of a ledger. The five analyses study 15, 22, 11, 28, and 19 cases, which sum to 95, and 95 divided by 5 is 19.0. The smallest analysis studies 11 cases and the largest studies 28. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with, and a design that cannot be checked is only a picture. Formal statement: total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts. Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('analysis 1', 15), ('analysis 2', 22), ('analysis 3', 11), ('analysis 4', 28), ('analysis 5', 19)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ProgJAHPRAIS000002:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        ledger = [('analysis 1', 15), ('analysis 2', 22), ('analysis 3', 11), ('analysis 4', 28), ('analysis 5', 19)]
        def compute():
            counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
            total = round(sum(counts), 4)
            mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
            smallest = min(counts)
            largest = max(counts)
            n = len(counts)
            out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}

Signature Decision Calculator: A Design for Expected Utility JAH-PRAIS-000003 · 3

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Decision Calculator is a Signature-made design for choosing by expected utility, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes the options, their outcomes, their probabilities, and their utilities, computes the expected utility of each, and reports the calculation with its sensitivity, so that the user can see how the choice changes when the inputs change. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of decision theory from von Neumann and Morgenstern in 1944 and Savage in 1954. The design takes the theory's clarity, that a consistent choice is a maximizing choice, and adds the sensitivity report: the calculator shows how the ranking changes when each probability and utility is varied, so the user can see which input decides the choice. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Decision Calculator is a Signature-made design for choosing by expected utility, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes the options, their outcomes, their probabilities, and their utilities, computes the expected utility of each, and reports the calculation with its sensitivity, so that the user can see how the choice changes when the inputs change. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Decision Calculator, a Signature-made design-study for expected-utility choice, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: list the options and their possible outcomes. Step 2: assign a probability to each outcome and a utility to each outcome, stating where each came from. Step 3: compute the expected utility of each option, and rank them. Step 4: report the ranking with the sensitivity, showing how it changes when the inputs change. The demo runs a small worked example of the choice step with illustrative design values: it takes five options with costs of 90, 140, 70, 180, and 110 and benefits of 270, 390, 230, 500, and 310, and reports the best option by benefit per cost, the total cost, and the total benefit. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"best_ratio_design": 3.2857, "total_cost_design": 590, "total_benefit_design": 1700, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the calculator computes, ranks, and reports the sensitivity, and the worked example shows the choice arithmetic. The benefit-per-cost ratios are 3.0, 2.7857, 3.2857, 2.7778, and 2.8182, so the best option is the third, with a ratio of 3.2857, and the totals are a cost of 590 and a benefit of 1700. The result that matters is the design judgment: a calculator that shows its sensitivity can be argued with, and one that reports only the ranking cannot, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: best: option 3, best_ratio: 3.2857, total_benefit: 1700, total_cost: 590.

When It Applies

The Signature Decision Calculator design applies as a study for anyone choosing under uncertainty: it shows how to compute, rank, and report the sensitivity. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the inputs are guesses treated as facts, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The calculator is a design, not a system, so it has never made a real decision, and its values are illustrative. The design assumes the probabilities and utilities can be stated honestly, which they often cannot be, and people violate the axioms, so the theory describes an ideal rather than a reality. The design is a study of how a decision calculator should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a calculator exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of ratios. The five options have benefit-per-cost ratios of 270 over 90, which is 3.0; 390 over 140, which is 2.7857; 230 over 70, which is 3.2857; 500 over 180, which is 2.7778; and 310 over 110, which is 2.8182. The best ratio is 3.2857, for the third option. The total cost is 90 plus 140 plus 70 plus 180 plus 110, which is 590, and the total benefit is 270 plus 390 plus 230 plus 500 plus 310, which is 1700. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: score = benefit / cost for each option; best = the option with the largest score; total cost = sum of costs; total benefit = sum of benefits. The equation states the choice check the demo performs: it scores every option by benefit divided by cost, picks the largest score as the best option, and totals the costs and benefits.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of rational choice under uncertainty: the axioms state what consistency requires, and the representation shows that a consistent choice is a maximizing choice. The design follows the public evidence that the theory is normative rather than descriptive, and it answers by making the inputs explicit, so that the user can see that the choice follows from the inputs rather than from the mathematics. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A prescriptive choice compares options by what each one returns for what it costs; the best option is the one with the largest benefit per unit of cost.. Working demo program

import hashlib
options = [('option 1', 90, 270), ('option 2', 140, 390), ('option 3', 70, 230), ('option 4', 180, 500), ('option 5', 110, 310)]
def compute():
    scored = [(b / c, name) for name, c, b in options]
    best_ratio_raw, best = max(scored)
    best_ratio = round(best_ratio_raw, 4)
    total_cost = sum(c for _, c, _ in options)
    total_benefit = sum(b for _, _, b in options)
    out = {"best": best, "best_ratio": best_ratio, "total_cost": total_cost, "total_benefit": total_benefit}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature MDP Solver: A Design for Sequential Choice JAH-PRAIS-000004 · 4

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature MDP Solver is a Signature-made design for solving sequential choice problems, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes the states, the actions, the rewards, and the transitions, solves the Bellman equation by iteration, and reads the optimal policy from the values, reporting the policy with the discount and the state that was assumed. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of Markov decision processes from Bellman in 1957. The design takes the recursion, that the value of a state depends on the value of the next state, and makes the reporting explicit: the solver shows the values, the policy, and the discount, so that the policy can be checked against the assumptions. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Worked Solution

Step 3: solve it by iteration, updating the values until they stop changing. Step 4: read the optimal policy from the values, and report it with the discount and the state. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements.

Working demo program

import hashlib
options = [('action 1', 18, 85), ('action 2', 32, 130), ('action 3', 14, 66), ('action 4', 42, 170), ('action 5', 24, 96)]
def compute():
    scored = [(b / c, name) for name, c, b in options]
    best_ratio_raw, best = max(scored)
    best_ratio = round(best_ratio_raw, 4)
    total_cost = sum(c for _, c, _ in options)
    total_benefit = sum(b for _, _, b in options)
    out = {"best": best, "best_ratio": best_ratio, "total_cost": total_cost, "total_benefit": total_benefit}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ProgJAHPRAIS000004:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        options = [('action 1', 18, 85), ('action 2', 32, 130), ('action 3', 14, 66), ('action 4', 42, 170), ('action 5', 24, 96)]
        def compute():
            scored = [(b / c, name) for name, c, b in options]
            best_ratio_raw, best = max(scored)
            best_ratio = round(best_ratio_raw, 4)
            total_cost = sum(c for _, c, _ in options)
            total_benefit = sum(b for _, _, b in options)
            out = {"best": best, "best_ratio": best_ratio, "total_cost": total_cost, "total_benefit": total_benefit}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}

Signature Recommender Designer: A Design for Prescribing with Consent JAH-PRAIS-000005 · 5

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Recommender Designer is a Signature-made design for prescribing what a person might like next, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design predicts the rating, ranks the items, and prescribes the top ones, but it adds the consent rule: the user can see why each item was prescribed, can dismiss it, and can ask for more diversity, so that the prescription serves the user rather than the platform. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of recommender systems from GroupLens in 1994 through the Netflix Prize of 2006 to 2009. The design takes the lesson of that history, that ensembles win and that a recommender which optimizes engagement can narrow what people see, and adds the diversity measure and the consent rule. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Recommender Designer is a Signature-made design for prescribing what a person might like next, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design predicts the rating, ranks the items, and prescribes the top ones, but it adds the consent rule: the user can see why each item was prescribed, can dismiss it, and can ask for more diversity, so that the prescription serves the user rather than the platform. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Recommender Designer, a Signature-made design-study for recommender systems, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take each user's history as ratings or interactions. Step 2: predict the rating for the unseen items, and rank them. Step 3: prescribe the top ones, with the reason for each and a diversity measure for the list. Step 4: let the user dismiss, adjust, and ask for more diversity, and measure the outcome. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step with illustrative design values: it takes five predicted ratings, 4.2, 3.4, 4.8, 2.8, and 3.9, against actual ratings of 4.0, 3.6, 4.6, 3.0, and 3.8, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mae_design": 0.18, "rmse_design": 0.1844, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the designer predicts, ranks, prescribes with reasons, and measures diversity, and the worked example shows the error arithmetic. The gaps are 0.2, 0.2, 0.2, 0.2, and 0.1, giving a mean absolute error of 0.18 and a root mean square error of 0.1844, with a largest gap of 0.2. The result that matters is the design judgment: a recommender that shows its reasons and measures its diversity can be trusted, and one that does neither cannot, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 0.18, max_err: 0.2, n: 5, rmse: 0.1844.

When It Applies

The Signature Recommender Designer design applies as a study for anyone building a recommender: it shows how to predict, rank, prescribe with reasons, and measure diversity. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the prescription would narrow what people see without consent, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The designer is a design, not a system, so it has never recommended a real item, and its ratings are illustrative. The design assumes the user wants the prescription, which is not always true, and it cannot know what a person would like if they tried it. The design is a study of how a recommender should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a designer exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 0.2, 0.2, 0.2, 0.2, and 0.1, which sum to 0.9, so the mean absolute error is 0.9 divided by 5, which is 0.18. The squared gaps are 0.04, 0.04, 0.04, 0.04, and 0.01, which sum to 0.17, so the mean squared error is 0.034 and the root mean square error is the square root of 0.034, which is 0.1844. The largest single gap is 0.2. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of similarity: people who agreed in the past will agree in the future, and items liked together will be liked together again. The design follows the public evidence that the ensemble wins and that the prescription must be judged by what it prescribes rather than only by how accurately it predicts, and it makes the diversity a measured quantity. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(4.2, 4.0), (3.4, 3.6), (4.8, 4.6), (2.8, 3.0), (3.9, 3.8)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature Logistics Router: A Design for Routes That Update JAH-PRAIS-000006 · 6

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Logistics Router is a Signature-made design for routing a fleet, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design builds an initial set of routes, improves them by local moves, and re-solves as traffic and availability change during the day, reporting the savings against the baseline at each step. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of the vehicle routing problem from Dantzig and Ramser in 1959. The design takes the heuristic idea, that a good route is found by improving a good route rather than by enumerating all of them, and adds the re-solve rule: the routes are recomputed whenever the conditions change by more than a stated threshold. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Logistics Router is a Signature-made design for routing a fleet, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design builds an initial set of routes, improves them by local moves, and re-solves as traffic and availability change during the day, reporting the savings against the baseline at each step. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the deliveries as stops with demands and time windows. Step 2: build an initial set of routes, respecting capacity. Step 3: improve the routes by local moves, swapping stops between routes and reordering within them. Step 4: re-solve as conditions change, and report the savings against the baseline. The demo runs a small worked example of the choice step with illustrative design values: it takes five route options with costs of 110, 88, 140, 75, and 100 and benefits of 280, 240, 350, 205, and 260, and reports the best option by benefit per cost, the total cost, and the total benefit. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The Answer

The benefit-per-cost ratios are 2.5455, 2.7273, 2.5, 2.7333, and 2.6, so the best option is the fourth, with a ratio of 2.7333, and the totals are a cost of 513 and a benefit of 1335. The result that matters is the design judgment: a router that re-solves as conditions change is honest, and one that plans once in the morning and never looks again is not, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The design is a study of how a router should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a router exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of ratios. The five options have benefit-per-cost ratios of 280 over 110, which is 2.5455; 240 over 88, which is 2.7273; 350 over 140, which is 2.5; 205 over 75, which is 2.7333; and 260 over 100, which is 2.6. The total cost is 110 plus 88 plus 140 plus 75 plus 100, which is 513, and the total benefit is 280 plus 240 plus 350 plus 205 plus 260, which is 1335. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: score = benefit / cost for each option; best = the option with the largest score; total cost = sum of costs; total benefit = sum of benefits. The equation states the choice check the demo performs: it scores every option by benefit divided by cost, picks the largest score as the best option, and totals the costs and benefits.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of combinatorial optimization: the number of possible routes grows faster than any computer can enumerate, so the exact optimum is out of reach for large problems, and the practical answer is a heuristic that finds a good route quickly. The design follows the public evidence that a good route is usually close to the optimum, and it adds the re-solve rule, because the conditions change during the day. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A prescriptive choice compares options by what each one returns for what it costs; the best option is the one with the largest benefit per unit of cost.. History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of the vehicle routing problem from Dantzig and Ramser in 1959. The design takes the heuristic idea, that a good route is found by improving a good route rather than by enumerating all of them, and adds the re-solve rule: the routes are recomputed whenever the conditions change by more than a stated threshold. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Problem

What is the Signature Logistics Router, a Signature-made design-study for routing that updates as the day unfolds, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

Method

Step 1: take the deliveries as stops with demands and time windows. Step 2: build an initial set of routes, respecting capacity. Step 3: improve the routes by local moves, swapping stops between routes and reordering within them. Step 4: re-solve as conditions change, and report the savings against the baseline. The demo runs a small worked example of the choice step with illustrative design values: it takes five route options with costs of 110, 88, 140, 75, and 100 and benefits of 280, 240, 350, 205, and 260, and reports the best option by benefit per cost, the total cost, and the total benefit. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements.

Working demo program

import hashlib
options = [('route 1', 110, 280), ('route 2', 88, 240), ('route 3', 140, 350), ('route 4', 75, 205), ('route 5', 100, 260)]
def compute():
    scored = [(b / c, name) for name, c, b in options]
    best_ratio_raw, best = max(scored)
    best_ratio = round(best_ratio_raw, 4)
    total_cost = sum(c for _, c, _ in options)
    total_benefit = sum(b for _, _, b in options)
    out = {"best": best, "best_ratio": best_ratio, "total_cost": total_cost, "total_benefit": total_benefit}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ProgJAHPRAIS000006:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        options = [('route 1', 110, 280), ('route 2', 88, 240), ('route 3', 140, 350), ('route 4', 75, 205), ('route 5', 100, 260)]
        def compute():
            scored = [(b / c, name) for name, c, b in options]
            best_ratio_raw, best = max(scored)
            best_ratio = round(best_ratio_raw, 4)
            total_cost = sum(c for _, c, _ in options)
            total_benefit = sum(b for _, _, b in options)
            out = {"best": best, "best_ratio": best_ratio, "total_cost": total_cost, "total_benefit": total_benefit}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature Treatment Planner: A Design for Advising, Not Deciding JAH-PRAIS-000007 · 7

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Treatment Planner is a Signature-made design for planning a treatment sequence, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design models the disease as a process with states, optimizes the treatment sequence, and reports the prescription with its evidence, its uncertainty, and its alternatives, leaving the decision to the clinician. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of decision analysis from the 1970s and Markov disease models from the 1980s and 1990s. The design takes the evidence discipline the public record shows, that a prescription must be supported by trials, and makes the evidence part of the report: every prescribed step carries its trial, its effect size, and its uncertainty. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Treatment Planner is a Signature-made design for planning a treatment sequence, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design models the disease as a process with states, optimizes the treatment sequence, and reports the prescription with its evidence, its uncertainty, and its alternatives, leaving the decision to the clinician. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Treatment Planner, a Signature-made design-study for treatment optimization, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the patient's state as input. Step 2: model the disease as a process with states and transitions, and the treatments as actions with outcomes. Step 3: optimize the treatment sequence, maximizing the expected outcome under the uncertainty. Step 4: report the prescription with its evidence, its uncertainty, and the alternatives, and let the clinician decide. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step with illustrative design values: it takes five treatment options with predicted outcome scores of 0.82, 0.7, 0.88, 0.63, and 0.78 against observed scores of 0.8, 0.72, 0.86, 0.66, and 0.76, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mae_design": 0.022, "rmse_design": 0.0224, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the planner models, optimizes, and reports the evidence, and the worked example shows the error arithmetic. The gaps are 0.02, 0.02, 0.02, 0.03, and 0.02, giving a mean absolute error of 0.022 and a root mean square error of 0.0224, with a largest gap of 0.03. The result that matters is the design judgment: a planner that reports its evidence and defers to the clinician is a tool, and one that decides on its own is not, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 0.022, max_err: 0.03, n: 5, rmse: 0.0224.

When It Applies

The Signature Treatment Planner design applies as a study for anyone building a treatment system: it shows how to model, optimize, and report the evidence, and how to defer to the clinician. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply as medical advice, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The planner is a design, not a system, so it has never planned a real treatment, and its scores are illustrative. The design assumes the evidence is strong and the model is validated, which is not always true, and the patient is an individual, so the prescription must be tailored. The design is a study of how a treatment planner should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a planner exists or works, or for medical advice of any kind.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 0.02, 0.02, 0.02, 0.03, and 0.02, which sum to 0.11, so the mean absolute error is 0.11 divided by 5, which is 0.022. The squared gaps are 0.0004, 0.0004, 0.0004, 0.0009, and 0.0004, which sum to 0.0025, so the mean squared error is 0.0005 and the root mean square error is the square root of 0.0005, which is 0.0224. The largest single gap is 0.03. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of evidence-based decision: a treatment's value is measured by trials, the disease's course is modeled as a process, and the prescription is the sequence that maximizes the expected outcome. The design follows the public evidence that a treatment which works on average may not work for this patient, and it answers by reporting the uncertainty and the alternatives alongside the prescription. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.82, 0.8), (0.7, 0.72), (0.88, 0.86), (0.63, 0.66), (0.78, 0.76)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature Energy Dispatcher: A Design for Balancing the Grid JAH-PRAIS-000008 · 8

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Energy Dispatcher is a Signature-made design for balancing a power grid, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design solves the unit commitment, choosing which generators to run, and the economic dispatch, choosing how much each should produce, and it re-solves as the forecast updates, keeping a reserve against the uncertainty. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of economic dispatch from the mid-twentieth century and unit commitment from the 1970s and 1980s. The design takes the balancing requirement, that supply must equal demand at every instant, and adds the reserve rule: the schedule always keeps a stated reserve, and the reserve is reported alongside the cost. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Energy Dispatcher is a Signature-made design for balancing a power grid, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design solves the unit commitment, choosing which generators to run, and the economic dispatch, choosing how much each should produce, and it re-solves as the forecast updates, keeping a reserve against the uncertainty. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Energy Dispatcher, a Signature-made design-study for grid optimization, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the demand forecast and the generator data as input. Step 2: solve the unit commitment, choosing which generators to run and when to start them. Step 3: solve the economic dispatch, choosing how much each running generator should produce. Step 4: report the schedule, the cost, and the reserve, and re-solve as the forecast updates. The demo runs a small worked example of the choice step with illustrative design values: it takes five generator options with costs of 55, 42, 75, 50, and 65 and benefits of 190, 150, 250, 180, and 220, and reports the best option by benefit per cost, the total cost, and the total benefit. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"best_ratio_design": 3.6, "total_cost_design": 287, "total_benefit_design": 990, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the dispatcher commits, dispatches, keeps a reserve, and re-solves, and the worked example shows the choice arithmetic. The benefit-per-cost ratios are 3.4545, 3.5714, 3.3333, 3.6, and 3.3846, so the best option is the fourth, with a ratio of 3.6, and the totals are a cost of 287 and a benefit of 990. The result that matters is the design judgment: a dispatcher that keeps and reports its reserve is honest, and one that cuts the reserve to save money is not, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: best: generator 4, best_ratio: 3.6, total_benefit: 990, total_cost: 287.

When It Applies

The Signature Energy Dispatcher design applies as a study for anyone building a grid optimizer: it shows how to commit, dispatch, keep a reserve, and re-solve. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the forecast is treated as certain, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The dispatcher is a design, not a system, so it has never balanced a real grid, and its values are illustrative. The design assumes the forecast is good enough to plan on, which is not always true, and a schedule that is optimal for the forecast may be poor for the reality. The design is a study of how a dispatcher should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a dispatcher exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of ratios. The five options have benefit-per-cost ratios of 190 over 55, which is 3.4545; 150 over 42, which is 3.5714; 250 over 75, which is 3.3333; 180 over 50, which is 3.6; and 220 over 65, which is 3.3846. The best ratio is 3.6, for the fourth option. The total cost is 55 plus 42 plus 75 plus 50 plus 65, which is 287, and the total benefit is 190 plus 150 plus 250 plus 180 plus 220, which is 990. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: score = benefit / cost for each option; best = the option with the largest score; total cost = sum of costs; total benefit = sum of benefits. The equation states the choice check the demo performs: it scores every option by benefit divided by cost, picks the largest score as the best option, and totals the costs and benefits.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of balancing under uncertainty: the grid must match supply to demand at every instant, the generators have different costs and speeds, and the demand and the renewable output are uncertain. The design follows the public evidence that the optimization is mixed-integer and hard, and that the reserve is the insurance against the uncertainty. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A prescriptive choice compares options by what each one returns for what it costs; the best option is the one with the largest benefit per unit of cost.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
options = [('generator 1', 55, 190), ('generator 2', 42, 150), ('generator 3', 75, 250), ('generator 4', 50, 180), ('generator 5', 65, 220)]
def compute():
    scored = [(b / c, name) for name, c, b in options]
    best_ratio_raw, best = max(scored)
    best_ratio = round(best_ratio_raw, 4)
    total_cost = sum(c for _, c, _ in options)
    total_benefit = sum(b for _, _, b in options)
    out = {"best": best, "best_ratio": best_ratio, "total_cost": total_cost, "total_benefit": total_benefit}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class ProgJAHPRAIS000008:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        options = [('generator 1', 55, 190), ('generator 2', 42, 150), ('generator 3', 75, 250), ('generator 4', 50, 180), ('generator 5', 65, 220)]
        def compute():
            scored = [(b / c, name) for name, c, b in options]
            best_ratio_raw, best = max(scored)
            best_ratio = round(best_ratio_raw, 4)
            total_cost = sum(c for _, c, _ in options)
            total_benefit = sum(b for _, _, b in options)
            out = {"best": best, "best_ratio": best_ratio, "total_cost": total_cost, "total_benefit": total_benefit}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHPRAIS000008().run())

Signature Simulation Studio: A Design for Rehearsing Decisions JAH-PRAIS-000009 · 9

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Simulation Studio is a Signature-made design for rehearsing a decision before taking it, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design builds a simulation of the system, runs it many times for each candidate action, and prescribes the action that does best on average without doing badly in the worst case, reporting the range alongside the average. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of Monte Carlo methods from the 1940s and digital twins from the 2010s. The design takes the rehearsal idea, that any system which can be simulated can be tested before it is changed, and adds the worst-case rule: the prescription must report the worst case alongside the average, because the average alone hides the risk. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Simulation Studio is a Signature-made design for rehearsing a decision before taking it, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design builds a simulation of the system, runs it many times for each candidate action, and prescribes the action that does best on average without doing badly in the worst case, reporting the range alongside the average. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Simulation Studio, a Signature-made design-study for simulation-based prescription, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: build a simulation of the system, with its rules, its randomness, and its inputs. Step 2: run the simulation many times for each candidate action, sampling the random inputs afresh each time. Step 3: compare the actions by their average outcome and by their worst case. Step 4: prescribe the action that does best on average without doing badly in the worst case, and report the range. The demo runs a small worked example of the counting step with illustrative design values: it takes five simulation batches with runs completed of 900, 1100, 800, 1400, and 1000, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"total_runs_design": 5200, "mean_runs_design": 1040.0, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the studio builds, runs, compares, and reports the worst case, and the worked example shows the counting arithmetic. The five batches complete 900, 1100, 800, 1400, and 1000 runs, for a total of 5200, a mean of 1040.0, a smallest of 800, and a largest of 1400. The result that matters is the design judgment: a rehearsal that reports its worst case is honest, and one that reports only its average is not, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 1400, mean: 1040.0, n: 5, smallest: 800, total: 5200.

When It Applies

The Signature Simulation Studio design applies as a study for anyone rehearsing a decision: it shows how to build, run, compare, and report the worst case. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the model is unvalidated, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The studio is a design, not a system, so it has never rehearsed a real decision, and its counts are illustrative. The design assumes the simulation is faithful, which is the hard part, and the runs are expensive, so the number needed grows with the rarity of the events that matter. The design is a study of how a rehearsal should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a studio exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of a ledger. The five batches complete 900, 1100, 800, 1400, and 1000 runs, which sum to 5200, and 5200 divided by 5 is 1040.0. The smallest batch completes 800 runs and the largest completes 1400. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with, and a design that cannot be checked is only a picture. Formal statement: total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts. The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of the law of large numbers: the average of many random runs converges to the expected outcome, and the spread of the runs shows the risk. The design follows the public evidence that simulation works where the model is faithful, and it adds the validation requirement: the simulation must be checked against the real system before its prescriptions are followed. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('batch 1', 900), ('batch 2', 1100), ('batch 3', 800), ('batch 4', 1400), ('batch 5', 1000)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ProgJAHPRAIS000009:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        ledger = [('batch 1', 900), ('batch 2', 1100), ('batch 3', 800), ('batch 4', 1400), ('batch 5', 1000)]
        def compute():
            counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
            total = round(sum(counts), 4)
            mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
            smallest = min(counts)
            largest = max(counts)
            n = len(counts)
            out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHPRAIS000009().run())

Signature Uplift Engine: A Design for Targeting the Persuadables JAH-PRAIS-000010 · 10

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Uplift Engine is a Signature-made design for targeting an action at the people whose decision it will change, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design runs a randomized trial, models the difference the action makes for each person, and prescribes the action to the persuadables, the ones whose decision the action changes, rather than to those who would act anyway. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of randomized trials, A/B testing in the 2000s, and uplift modelling in the 2010s. The design takes the causal lesson, that prediction and prescription are different questions, and makes the trial part of the design: no uplift estimate is reported without the trial that produced it. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Uplift Engine is a Signature-made design for targeting an action at the people whose decision it will change, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design runs a randomized trial, models the difference the action makes for each person, and prescribes the action to the persuadables, the ones whose decision the action changes, rather than to those who would act anyway. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Uplift Engine, a Signature-made design-study for causal targeting, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: run a randomized trial, treating a random half and leaving the other half untreated. Step 2: model the outcome in both groups, and compute the uplift for each person. Step 3: rank the people by uplift, and prescribe the action to the top of the list. Step 4: measure the outcome in a fresh trial, and report the gain over targeting by prediction alone. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step with illustrative design values: it takes five uplift estimates, 0.11, 0.07, 0.14, 0.04, and 0.09, against measured uplifts of 0.1, 0.08, 0.12, 0.05, and 0.1, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mae_design": 0.012, "rmse_design": 0.0126, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the engine trials, models, ranks, and measures the gain, and the worked example shows the error arithmetic. The gaps are 0.01, 0.01, 0.02, 0.01, and 0.01, giving a mean absolute error of 0.012 and a root mean square error of 0.0126, with a largest gap of 0.02. The result that matters is the design judgment: an engine that measures its gain over prediction alone is honest, and one that reports only its accuracy is not, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 0.012, max_err: 0.02, n: 5, rmse: 0.0126.

When It Applies

The Signature Uplift Engine design applies as a study for anyone targeting an action: it shows how to trial, model, rank, and measure the gain. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where no trial was run, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The engine is a design, not a system, so it has never run a real trial, and its values are illustrative. The design assumes the trial is truly random and the sample is large enough, which is expensive, and the estimate is noisy. The design is a study of how causal targeting should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such an engine exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 0.01, 0.01, 0.02, 0.01, and 0.01, which sum to 0.06, so the mean absolute error is 0.06 divided by 5, which is 0.012. The squared gaps are 0.0001, 0.0001, 0.0004, 0.0001, and 0.0001, which sum to 0.0008, so the mean squared error is 0.00016 and the root mean square error is the square root of 0.00016, which is 0.0126. The largest single gap is 0.02. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of causation: a randomized trial separates the effect of the action from the characteristics of the people who receive it, because the treatment is assigned by chance. The design follows the public evidence that targeting the likely buyers wastes money on people who needed no persuading, and it answers by ranking on the difference the action makes. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.11, 0.1), (0.07, 0.08), (0.14, 0.12), (0.04, 0.05), (0.09, 0.1)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ProgJAHPRAIS000010:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(0.11, 0.1), (0.07, 0.08), (0.14, 0.12), (0.04, 0.05), (0.09, 0.1)]
        def compute():
            errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
            mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
            rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
            max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
            n = len(pairs)
            out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
