Signature Pareto Explorer: A Design for Showing Trade-Offs JAH-PRAIS-000011 · 11

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Pareto Explorer is a Signature-made design for finding and showing the trade-off between conflicting goals, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design searches for the Pareto frontier, the options where no goal can be improved without worsening another, and presents it with the trade-offs visible, leaving the choice along the frontier to the decision maker. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of Pareto efficiency from 1906. The design takes the frontier idea, and adds the recording rule: the choice along the frontier is recorded with its reasons, so that the decision can be revisited and defended. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Pareto Explorer is a Signature-made design for finding and showing the trade-off between conflicting goals, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design searches for the Pareto frontier, the options where no goal can be improved without worsening another, and presents it with the trade-offs visible, leaving the choice along the frontier to the decision maker. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Pareto Explorer, a Signature-made design-study for multi-objective optimization, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the objectives and the constraints. Step 2: search for the Pareto frontier, the options where no objective can be improved without worsening another. Step 3: present the frontier, with the trade-offs visible. Step 4: let the decision maker choose along the frontier, and record the choice and its reasons. The demo runs a small worked example of the choice step with illustrative design values: it takes five frontier options with costs of 45, 65, 85, 105, and 125 and benefits of 95, 125, 150, 170, and 185, and reports the best option by benefit per cost, the total cost, and the total benefit. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"best_ratio_design": 2.1111, "total_cost_design": 425, "total_benefit_design": 725, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the explorer searches, presents, and records the choice, and the worked example shows the choice arithmetic. The benefit-per-cost ratios are 2.1111, 1.9231, 1.7647, 1.619, and 1.48, so the best option by ratio is the first, with a ratio of 2.1111, and the totals are a cost of 425 and a benefit of 725. The result that matters is the design judgment: an explorer that shows the frontier and records the choice is honest, and one that picks a point without showing the frontier is not, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: best: option 1, best_ratio: 2.1111, total_benefit: 725, total_cost: 425.

When It Applies

The Signature Pareto Explorer design applies as a study for anyone deciding between conflicting goals: it shows how to search, present, and record the choice. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where an objective is left out, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The explorer is a design, not a system, so it has never found a real frontier, and its values are illustrative. The design assumes the objectives can be stated, which is not always true, and the search can miss parts of the frontier, especially where it is discontinuous. The design is a study of how a trade-off should be shown, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such an explorer exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of ratios. The five options have benefit-per-cost ratios of 95 over 45, which is 2.1111; 125 over 65, which is 1.9231; 150 over 85, which is 1.7647; 170 over 105, which is 1.619; and 185 over 125, which is 1.48. The best ratio is 2.1111, for the first option. The total cost is 45 plus 65 plus 85 plus 105 plus 125, which is 425, and the total benefit is 95 plus 125 plus 150 plus 170 plus 185, which is 725. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: score = benefit / cost for each option; best = the option with the largest score; total cost = sum of costs; total benefit = sum of benefits. The equation states the choice check the demo performs: it scores every option by benefit divided by cost, picks the largest score as the best option, and totals the costs and benefits.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of trade-offs: when goals conflict, the best that can be done is to find the options that waste nothing, and the frontier is exactly that set. The design follows the public evidence that the mathematics can find the frontier but cannot choose along it, because the choice is about what matters, and it makes the recording of the choice part of the design. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A prescriptive choice compares options by what each one returns for what it costs; the best option is the one with the largest benefit per unit of cost.. Working demo program

import hashlib
options = [('option 1', 45, 95), ('option 2', 65, 125), ('option 3', 85, 150), ('option 4', 105, 170), ('option 5', 125, 185)]
def compute():
    scored = [(b / c, name) for name, c, b in options]
    best_ratio_raw, best = max(scored)
    best_ratio = round(best_ratio_raw, 4)
    total_cost = sum(c for _, c, _ in options)
    total_benefit = sum(b for _, _, b in options)
    out = {"best": best, "best_ratio": best_ratio, "total_cost": total_cost, "total_benefit": total_benefit}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature Constraint Solver: A Design for Hard Rules JAH-PRAIS-000012 · 12

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Constraint Solver is a Signature-made design for finding an assignment that satisfies every hard rule, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design propagates the constraints to narrow the possibilities, searches by choosing a variable and trying a value, backtracks when a constraint is violated, and proves feasibility or infeasibility. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of constraint programming from the 1970s and 1980s. The design takes the propagation idea, that a constraint which must hold narrows the possibilities, and adds the proof: the solver reports whether a feasible assignment exists, and proves it either way, so that an infeasible problem is not mistaken for an unsolved one. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Constraint Solver is a Signature-made design for finding an assignment that satisfies every hard rule, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design propagates the constraints to narrow the possibilities, searches by choosing a variable and trying a value, backtracks when a constraint is violated, and proves feasibility or infeasibility. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Constraint Solver, a Signature-made design-study for scheduling under hard rules, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the variables, their domains, and the constraints that must hold. Step 2: propagate the constraints, narrowing each variable's domain. Step 3: search by choosing a variable and trying a value, backtracking when a constraint is violated. Step 4: report the feasible assignment, or prove that none exists. The demo runs a small worked example of the counting step with illustrative design values: it takes five solving runs with constraints checked of 220, 290, 170, 270, and 240, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"total_constraints_design": 1190, "mean_constraints_design": 238.0, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the solver propagates, searches, backtracks, and proves, and the worked example shows the counting arithmetic. The five runs check 220, 290, 170, 270, and 240 constraints, for a total of 1190, a mean of 238.0, a smallest of 170, and a largest of 290. The result that matters is the design judgment: a solver that proves feasibility or infeasibility is honest, and one that merely fails to find an assignment is not, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 290, mean: 238.0, n: 5, smallest: 170, total: 1190.

When It Applies

The Signature Constraint Solver design applies as a study for anyone scheduling under hard rules: it shows how to propagate, search, backtrack, and prove. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the constraints are soft, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The solver is a design, not a system, so it has never solved a real scheduling problem, and its counts are illustrative. The design assumes the constraints are complete, which they often are not, and a problem with weak constraints will take too long to search. The design is a study of how a constraint solver should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a solver exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of a ledger. The five runs check 220, 290, 170, 270, and 240 constraints, which sum to 1190, and 1190 divided by 5 is 238.0. The smallest run checks 170 constraints and the largest checks 290. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with, and a design that cannot be checked is only a picture. Formal statement: total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts. The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of propagation: a constraint that must hold narrows the possibilities, and the narrowing compounds, so the search space shrinks before the search begins. The design follows the public evidence that strong constraints prune hard and weak ones do not, and it makes the proof part of the design. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('run 1', 220), ('run 2', 290), ('run 3', 170), ('run 4', 270), ('run 5', 240)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature RL Prescriber: A Design for Learning Policies by Trial JAH-PRAIS-000013 · 13

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature RL Prescriber is a Signature-made design for learning a policy by trial, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design lets an agent try actions, observe the rewards, and learn a policy that maximizes the total reward, with the exploration-exploitation balance stated and the learned policy tested in fresh episodes before it is used. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of reinforcement learning from Bellman in 1957 through Q-learning in 1989 to AlphaGo in 2016. The design takes the trial-and-error idea, and adds the transfer rule: a policy learned in simulation is never used in the world until it has been tested there, because the simulation leaves out what the world contains. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature RL Prescriber is a Signature-made design for learning a policy by trial, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design lets an agent try actions, observe the rewards, and learn a policy that maximizes the total reward, with the exploration-exploitation balance stated and the learned policy tested in fresh episodes before it is used. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature RL Prescriber, a Signature-made design-study for reinforcement learning policies, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the states, the actions, and the rewards, with a discount for the future. Step 2: let the agent try actions, observe the rewards, and update its estimates of each action's value. Step 3: improve the policy toward the actions with the highest estimated value, and keep exploring so the estimates stay honest. Step 4: test the learned policy in fresh episodes, and report its total reward. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step with illustrative design values: it takes five episode rewards, 110, 140, 125, 155, and 135, against baseline rewards of 95, 105, 100, 110, and 103, and reports the mean absolute gap and the root mean square gap. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mae_gap_design": 30.4, "rmse_gap_design": 32.0125, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the prescriber tries, learns, tests fresh, and reports the total reward, and the worked example shows the comparison arithmetic. The gaps are 15, 35, 25, 45, and 32, giving a mean absolute gap of 30.4 and a root mean square gap of 32.0125, with a largest gap of 45. The result that matters is the design judgment: a policy that beats the baseline on fresh episodes has learned something real, and one that beats it only on its training episodes has not, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 30.4, max_err: 45, n: 5, rmse: 32.0125.

When It Applies

The Signature RL Prescriber design applies as a study for anyone learning a policy by trial: it shows how to try, learn, test fresh, and report the total reward. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the trying is expensive, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The prescriber is a design, not a system, so it has never learned a real policy, and its rewards are illustrative. The design assumes the trying is cheap and the rewards are honest, which is not always true, and an agent will optimize exactly what it is rewarded for, including the loopholes. The design is a study of how a learned policy should be built and tested, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a prescriber exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 15, 35, 25, 45, and 32, which sum to 152, so the mean absolute gap is 152 divided by 5, which is 30.4. The squared gaps are 225, 1225, 625, 2025, and 1024, which sum to 5124, so the mean squared gap is 1024.8 and the root mean square gap is the square root of 1024.8, which is 32.0125. The largest single gap is 45. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of learning from reward: an agent that tries actions and observes rewards can estimate the value of each action, and the estimates improve with experience. The design follows the public evidence that the method works when the trying is cheap and the rewards are honest, and it adds the transfer rule, because a policy that works in simulation and fails in the world is not a policy at all. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(110, 95), (140, 105), (125, 100), (155, 110), (135, 103)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ProgJAHPRAIS000013:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(110, 95), (140, 105), (125, 100), (155, 110), (135, 103)]
        def compute():
            errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
            mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
            rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
            max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
            n = len(pairs)
            out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature Inventory Optimizer: A Design for Ordering What Is Needed JAH-PRAIS-000014 · 14

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Inventory Optimizer is a Signature-made design for deciding how much to order and when, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design balances the ordering cost against the holding cost for steady demand, and balances the cost of ordering too much against the cost of ordering too little for uncertain demand, reporting the prescription with the demand estimate it rests on. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of inventory theory from Harris's economic order quantity in 1913. The design takes the trade-off idea, and adds the re-solve rule: the prescription is recomputed whenever the demand estimate changes by more than a stated amount, because a prescription based on last year's demand is a prescription for last year's warehouse. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Inventory Optimizer is a Signature-made design for deciding how much to order and when, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design balances the ordering cost against the holding cost for steady demand, and balances the cost of ordering too much against the cost of ordering too little for uncertain demand, reporting the prescription with the demand estimate it rests on. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Inventory Optimizer, a Signature-made design-study for inventory optimization, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the demand, the ordering cost, and the holding cost. Step 2: compute the order quantity that balances the two costs. Step 3: for uncertain demand, compute the quantity from the demand distribution and the two costs of being wrong. Step 4: report the prescription with the demand estimate, and re-solve when the estimate changes. The demo runs a small worked example of the choice step with illustrative design values: it takes five order options with costs of 190, 250, 170, 310, and 230 and benefits of 480, 600, 440, 740, and 560, and reports the best option by benefit per cost, the total cost, and the total benefit. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"best_ratio_design": 2.5882, "total_cost_design": 1150, "total_benefit_design": 2820, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the optimizer balances, prescribes, and re-solves, and the worked example shows the choice arithmetic. The benefit-per-cost ratios are 2.5263, 2.4, 2.5882, 2.3871, and 2.4348, so the best option is the third, with a ratio of 2.5882, and the totals are a cost of 1150 and a benefit of 2820. The result that matters is the design judgment: an optimizer that re-solves when the demand changes is honest, and one that follows last year's prescription is not, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: best: order 3, best_ratio: 2.5882, total_benefit: 2820, total_cost: 1150.

When It Applies

The Signature Inventory Optimizer design applies as a study for anyone ordering stock: it shows how to balance, prescribe, and re-solve. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the demand is unknown, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The optimizer is a design, not a system, so it has never ordered real stock, and its values are illustrative. The design assumes steady demand and known costs, which real demand and costs often are not, and it cannot foresee a demand that has no precedent. The design is a study of how an inventory optimizer should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such an optimizer exists or works.

Math Proof

The best ratio is 2.5882, for the third option. The total cost is 190 plus 250 plus 170 plus 310 plus 230, which is 1150, and the total benefit is 480 plus 600 plus 440 plus 740 plus 560, which is 2820. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: score = benefit / cost for each option; best = the option with the largest score; total cost = sum of costs; total benefit = sum of benefits. The equation states the choice check the demo performs: it scores every option by benefit divided by cost, picks the largest score as the best option, and totals the costs and benefits.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of the trade-off: ordering often costs much in ordering and little in holding, ordering rarely costs little in ordering and much in holding, and the optimum balances the two. The design follows the public evidence that the economic order quantity's square root is the signature of that balance, and it adds the re-solve rule, because the demand estimate is the hard part. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Working demo program

import hashlib
options = [('order 1', 190, 480), ('order 2', 250, 600), ('order 3', 170, 440), ('order 4', 310, 740), ('order 5', 230, 560)]
def compute():
    scored = [(b / c, name) for name, c, b in options]
    best_ratio_raw, best = max(scored)
    best_ratio = round(best_ratio_raw, 4)
    total_cost = sum(c for _, c, _ in options)
    total_benefit = sum(b for _, _, b in options)
    out = {"best": best, "best_ratio": best_ratio, "total_cost": total_cost, "total_benefit": total_benefit}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature Scheduler: A Design for Orders That Meet Deadlines JAH-PRAIS-000015 · 15

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Scheduler is a Signature-made design for ordering jobs on shared machines so that deadlines are met, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes the jobs, their processing times, and their deadlines, chooses an order, and reports the completion time with the jobs that miss their deadlines, re-solving as conditions change. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of scheduling from Johnson's rule in 1954. The design takes the ordering idea, that the same jobs on the same machines can finish hours apart depending on the order, and adds the slack rule: the schedule keeps slack for the unexpected, and the slack is reported alongside the completion time. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Scheduler is a Signature-made design for ordering jobs on shared machines so that deadlines are met, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes the jobs, their processing times, and their deadlines, chooses an order, and reports the completion time with the jobs that miss their deadlines, re-solving as conditions change. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

What is the Signature Scheduler, a Signature-made design-study for scheduling optimization, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the jobs, their processing times on each machine, and their deadlines. Step 2: choose an order, by rule or by heuristic. Step 3: compute the completion time, and check the deadlines. Step 4: report the schedule, the completion time, the slack, and the jobs that miss their deadlines. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step with illustrative design values: it takes five scheduled completion times, 40, 36, 43, 38, and 42 hours, against target times of 38, 38, 38, 38, and 38 hours, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mae_design": 2.6, "rmse_design": 3.1305, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the scheduler orders, checks, keeps slack, and reports the misses, and the worked example shows the error arithmetic. The gaps are 2, 2, 5, 0, and 4, giving a mean absolute error of 2.6 and a root mean square error of 3.1305, with a largest gap of 5. The result that matters is the design judgment: a schedule that reports its slack and its misses is honest, and one that reports only its completion time is not, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 2.6, max_err: 5, n: 5, rmse: 3.1305.

When It Applies

The Signature Scheduler design applies as a study for anyone scheduling jobs: it shows how to order, check, keep slack, and report the misses. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the times are unknown, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The scheduler is a design, not a system, so it has never scheduled a real job, and its times are illustrative. The design assumes the processing times are known, which they often are not, and machines break and jobs arrive unexpectedly. The design is a study of how a scheduler should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a scheduler exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 2, 2, 5, 0, and 4, which sum to 13, so the mean absolute error is 13 divided by 5, which is 2.6. The squared gaps are 4, 4, 25, 0, and 16, which sum to 49, so the mean squared error is 9.8 and the root mean square error is the square root of 9.8, which is 3.1305. The largest single gap is 5. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of ordering: the completion time of a set of jobs depends on the order they run, and the right order minimizes the waiting. The design follows the public evidence that Johnson's rule proves the point for two machines and that heuristics serve the harder cases, and it adds the slack, because a schedule that cannot adapt will fail. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of scheduling from Johnson's rule in 1954. The design takes the ordering idea, that the same jobs on the same machines can finish hours apart depending on the order, and adds the slack rule: the schedule keeps slack for the unexpected, and the slack is reported alongside the completion time. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Problem

What is the Signature Scheduler, a Signature-made design-study for scheduling optimization, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

Method

Step 1: state the jobs, their processing times on each machine, and their deadlines. Step 2: choose an order, by rule or by heuristic. Step 3: compute the completion time, and check the deadlines. Step 4: report the schedule, the completion time, the slack, and the jobs that miss their deadlines. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step with illustrative design values: it takes five scheduled completion times, 40, 36, 43, 38, and 42 hours, against target times of 38, 38, 38, 38, and 38 hours, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements.

Formal equation

MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|

Formal equation, in plain words

The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Formal status

stated, not machine-checked

Scientific equation

A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.

Results reported

The gaps are 2, 2, 5, 0, and 4, giving a mean absolute error of 2.6 and a root mean square error of 3.1305, with a largest gap of 5. The result that matters is the design judgment: a schedule that reports its slack and its misses is honest, and one that reports only its completion time is not, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 2.6, max_err: 5, n: 5, rmse: 3.1305.

Skeptical analysis

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The scheduler is a design, not a system, so it has never scheduled a real job, and its times are illustrative. The design is a study of how a scheduler should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a scheduler exists or works.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(40, 38), (36, 38), (43, 38), (38, 38), (42, 38)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ProgJAHPRAIS000015:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(40, 38), (36, 38), (43, 38), (38, 38), (42, 38)]
        def compute():
            errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
            mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
            rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
            max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
            n = len(pairs)
            out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}

Signature Portfolio Optimizer: A Design for the Efficient Frontier JAH-PRAIS-000016 · 16

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Portfolio Optimizer is a Signature-made design for choosing a mix of assets, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design estimates each asset's return, risk, and correlation, computes the efficient frontier, and lets the investor choose along it, reporting the mix, the expected return, and the risk, with the estimates stated. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of modern portfolio theory from Markowitz in 1952. The design takes the frontier idea, and adds the robustness rule: the mix is tested out of sample, and the test is reported alongside the frontier, so that the investor can see whether the optimization survives contact with new data. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Portfolio Optimizer is a Signature-made design for choosing a mix of assets, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design estimates each asset's return, risk, and correlation, computes the efficient frontier, and lets the investor choose along it, reporting the mix, the expected return, and the risk, with the estimates stated. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Portfolio Optimizer, a Signature-made design-study for portfolio optimization, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: estimate each asset's expected return, risk, and correlation with the others. Step 2: compute the efficient frontier, the portfolios where no return can be gained without more risk. Step 3: choose along the frontier according to the investor's tolerance for risk. Step 4: test the mix out of sample, and report the mix, the return, the risk, and the test. The demo runs a small worked example of the choice step with illustrative design values: it takes five portfolio options with costs of 85, 115, 70, 145, and 95 and benefits of 170, 220, 155, 260, and 190, and reports the best option by benefit per cost, the total cost, and the total benefit. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"best_ratio_design": 2.2143, "total_cost_design": 510, "total_benefit_design": 995, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the optimizer estimates, computes the frontier, tests out of sample, and reports, and the worked example shows the choice arithmetic. The benefit-per-cost ratios are 2.0, 1.913, 2.2143, 1.7931, and 2.0, so the best option is the third, with a ratio of 2.2143, and the totals are a cost of 510 and a benefit of 995. The result that matters is the design judgment: a mix that is tested out of sample can be trusted, and one that is not cannot, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: best: portfolio 3, best_ratio: 2.2143, total_benefit: 995, total_cost: 510.

When It Applies

The Signature Portfolio Optimizer design applies as a study for anyone building a portfolio tool: it shows how to estimate, compute the frontier, test out of sample, and report. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply as financial advice, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The optimizer is a design, not a system, so it has never chosen a real portfolio, and its values are illustrative. The design assumes the estimates are stable, which they often are not, and it assumes the investor cares only about mean and variance, which is not true of real investors. The design is a study of how a portfolio optimizer should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such an optimizer exists or works, or for financial advice of any kind.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of ratios. The five options have benefit-per-cost ratios of 170 over 85, which is 2.0; 220 over 115, which is 1.913; 155 over 70, which is 2.2143; 260 over 145, which is 1.7931; and 190 over 95, which is 2.0. The best ratio is 2.2143, for the third option. The total cost is 85 plus 115 plus 70 plus 145 plus 95, which is 510, and the total benefit is 170 plus 220 plus 155 plus 260 plus 190, which is 995. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: score = benefit / cost for each option; best = the option with the largest score; total cost = sum of costs; total benefit = sum of benefits. The equation states the choice check the demo performs: it scores every option by benefit divided by cost, picks the largest score as the best option, and totals the costs and benefits.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of diversification: the risk of a portfolio depends on how its assets move together, and assets that do not move together cancel each other's swings. The design follows the public evidence that the optimizer will exploit noise in the estimates, and it answers with the out-of-sample test. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A prescriptive choice compares options by what each one returns for what it costs; the best option is the one with the largest benefit per unit of cost.. Working demo program

import hashlib
options = [('portfolio 1', 85, 170), ('portfolio 2', 115, 220), ('portfolio 3', 70, 155), ('portfolio 4', 145, 260), ('portfolio 5', 95, 190)]
def compute():
    scored = [(b / c, name) for name, c, b in options]
    best_ratio_raw, best = max(scored)
    best_ratio = round(best_ratio_raw, 4)
    total_cost = sum(c for _, c, _ in options)
    total_benefit = sum(b for _, _, b in options)
    out = {"best": best, "best_ratio": best_ratio, "total_cost": total_cost, "total_benefit": total_benefit}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature Dynamic Pricer: A Design for Prices That Adapt JAH-PRAIS-000017 · 17

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Dynamic Pricer is a Signature-made design for setting a price that adapts as the selling window closes, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design forecasts the demand at each candidate price, computes the expected revenue for each, and chooses the price with the highest expected revenue, re-solving as bookings arrive and reporting the revenue against the fixed-price baseline. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of revenue management from the airlines in the 1980s. The design takes the perishable-capacity idea, that a seat which flies empty earns nothing, and adds the fairness rule: the pricing must be explainable, and the segmentation must be stated, so that a price which changes by the minute can be defended. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Worked Solution

Step 3: choose the price with the highest expected revenue, subject to the capacity and the time left. Step 4: re-solve as bookings arrive, and report the revenue against the fixed-price baseline. The demo runs a small worked example of the choice step with illustrative design values: it takes five price options with costs of 28, 38, 24, 48, and 33 and benefits of 115, 145, 100, 175, and 135, and reports the best option by benefit per cost, the total cost, and the total benefit. The Answer

The Signature Dynamic Pricer design applies as a study for anyone building a pricing system: it shows how to forecast, compute, choose, and re-solve, with the segmentation stated. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the pricing would be unfair, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The pricer is a design, not a system, so it has never priced a real seat, and its values are illustrative. The design assumes the demand can be forecast, which is not always true, and a price that changes by the minute can feel unfair. The design is a study of how a dynamic pricer should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a pricer exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of ratios. The five options have benefit-per-cost ratios of 115 over 28, which is 4.1071; 145 over 38, which is 3.8158; 100 over 24, which is 4.1667; 175 over 48, which is 3.6458; and 135 over 33, which is 4.0909. The best ratio is 4.1667, for the third option. The total cost is 28 plus 38 plus 24 plus 48 plus 33, which is 171, and the total benefit is 115 plus 145 plus 100 plus 175 plus 135, which is 670. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: score = benefit / cost for each option; best = the option with the largest score; total cost = sum of costs; total benefit = sum of benefits. The equation states the choice check the demo performs: it scores every option by benefit divided by cost, picks the largest score as the best option, and totals the costs and benefits.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of perishable capacity: a seat, a room, or a car that goes unsold earns nothing, and the price must be set to sell the capacity before it perishes. The design follows the public evidence that the optimal price balances the margin against the volume, and that the balance shifts as the deadline approaches. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A prescriptive choice compares options by what each one returns for what it costs; the best option is the one with the largest benefit per unit of cost.. Problem

What is the Signature Dynamic Pricer, a Signature-made design-study for dynamic pricing, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

Method

Step 1: forecast the demand at each candidate price, from the history and the current bookings. Step 2: compute the expected revenue for each price, as the price times the expected sales. Step 3: choose the price with the highest expected revenue, subject to the capacity and the time left. Step 4: re-solve as bookings arrive, and report the revenue against the fixed-price baseline. The demo runs a small worked example of the choice step with illustrative design values: it takes five price options with costs of 28, 38, 24, 48, and 33 and benefits of 115, 145, 100, 175, and 135, and reports the best option by benefit per cost, the total cost, and the total benefit. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements.

Working demo program

import hashlib
options = [('price 1', 28, 115), ('price 2', 38, 145), ('price 3', 24, 100), ('price 4', 48, 175), ('price 5', 33, 135)]
def compute():
    scored = [(b / c, name) for name, c, b in options]
    best_ratio_raw, best = max(scored)
    best_ratio = round(best_ratio_raw, 4)
    total_cost = sum(c for _, c, _ in options)
    total_benefit = sum(b for _, _, b in options)
    out = {"best": best, "best_ratio": best_ratio, "total_cost": total_cost, "total_benefit": total_benefit}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ProgJAHPRAIS000017:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        options = [('price 1', 28, 115), ('price 2', 38, 145), ('price 3', 24, 100), ('price 4', 48, 175), ('price 5', 33, 135)]
        def compute():
            scored = [(b / c, name) for name, c, b in options]
            best_ratio_raw, best = max(scored)
            best_ratio = round(best_ratio_raw, 4)
            total_cost = sum(c for _, c, _ in options)
            total_benefit = sum(b for _, _, b in options)
            out = {"best": best, "best_ratio": best_ratio, "total_cost": total_cost, "total_benefit": total_benefit}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}

Signature Oversight Console: A Design for Keeping Humans in the Loop JAH-PRAIS-000018 · 18

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Oversight Console is a Signature-made design for keeping a human in the loop of a prescriptive system, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design shows every prescription with its reasoning, its evidence, and its uncertainty, makes the override easy, and measures the override rate, so that the loop can be seen to be alive. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of advisory expert systems and human-in-the-loop design. The design takes the lesson of that history, that a system which decides without oversight makes mistakes nobody catches, and makes the override rate a visible number on the console, so that a rate near zero is a warning rather than a success. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Oversight Console is a Signature-made design for keeping a human in the loop of a prescriptive system, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design shows every prescription with its reasoning, its evidence, and its uncertainty, makes the override easy, and measures the override rate, so that the loop can be seen to be alive. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Oversight Console, a Signature-made design-study for human oversight of prescription, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: present the prescription with its reasoning, its evidence, and its uncertainty. Step 2: let the human accept, modify, or reject it, and record the decision. Step 3: measure the override rate, and display it on the console. Step 4: investigate when the rate is very high or very low, and use the overrides to improve the system. The demo runs a small worked example of the counting step with illustrative design values: it takes five weeks with overrides of 11, 16, 8, 14, and 10 out of 100 prescriptions, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"total_overrides_design": 59, "mean_overrides_design": 11.8, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the console presents, records, measures, and investigates, and the worked example shows the counting arithmetic. The five weeks record 11, 16, 8, 14, and 10 overrides, for a total of 59, a mean of 11.8, a smallest of 8, and a largest of 16. The result that matters is the design judgment: an override rate of about twelve percent says the human is engaged, and a rate near zero would say the human has stopped checking, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 16, mean: 11.8, n: 5, smallest: 8, total: 59.

It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply as a formality, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

The design assumes the human has the time and the information to check, which is often false, and a human who must check a hundred prescriptions a day will stop checking. The design is a study of how oversight should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a console exists or works.

Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('week 1', 11), ('week 2', 16), ('week 3', 8), ('week 4', 14), ('week 5', 10)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature Prescriptive Maintenance Planner: A Design for Acting Before Failure JAH-PRAIS-000019 · 19

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Prescriptive Maintenance Planner is a Signature-made design for prescribing a repair before the failure, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design predicts the remaining useful life, optimizes the maintenance schedule, and prescribes the repair, the timing, and the parts, reporting the failures prevented and the maintenance saved. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of predictive maintenance from the 2010s and the prescriptive step that followed. The design takes the combination the public record shows, that prediction plus optimization is worth more than either alone, and makes the outcome the measure: the planner is judged by the failures prevented and the maintenance saved, not by the accuracy of the prediction alone. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Prescriptive Maintenance Planner is a Signature-made design for prescribing a repair before the failure, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design predicts the remaining useful life, optimizes the maintenance schedule, and prescribes the repair, the timing, and the parts, reporting the failures prevented and the maintenance saved. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Prescriptive Maintenance Planner, a Signature-made design-study for prescribing repairs, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: predict the remaining useful life from the sensor data. Step 2: optimize the maintenance schedule, choosing the time and the action that minimize the total cost. Step 3: prescribe the repair, the timing, and the parts, with the reasoning shown. Step 4: measure the outcome, the failures prevented and the maintenance saved, and update the model. The demo runs a small worked example of the choice step with illustrative design values: it takes five maintenance options with costs of 450, 650, 380, 850, and 550 and benefits of 1400, 1800, 1180, 2300, and 1650, and reports the best option by benefit per cost, the total cost, and the total benefit. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"best_ratio_design": 3.1111, "total_cost_design": 2880, "total_benefit_design": 8330, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the planner predicts, optimizes, prescribes, and measures the outcome, and the worked example shows the choice arithmetic. The benefit-per-cost ratios are 3.1111, 2.7692, 3.1053, 2.7059, and 3.0, so the best option is the first, with a ratio of 3.1111, and the totals are a cost of 2880 and a benefit of 8330. The result that matters is the design judgment: a planner that measures its outcome is useful, and one that reports only its prediction accuracy is not, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: best: maintenance 1, best_ratio: 3.1111, total_benefit: 8330, total_cost: 2880.

When It Applies

The Signature Prescriptive Maintenance Planner design applies as a study for anyone building a maintenance system: it shows how to predict, optimize, prescribe, and measure the outcome. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the prediction is unreliable, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The planner is a design, not a system, so it has never prescribed a real repair, and its values are illustrative. The design assumes the prediction is accurate and the costs are known, which is not always true, and the prescription needs the parts and the people to be available. The design is a study of how a maintenance planner should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a planner exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of ratios. The five options have benefit-per-cost ratios of 1400 over 450, which is 3.1111; 1800 over 650, which is 2.7692; 1180 over 380, which is 3.1053; 2300 over 850, which is 2.7059; and 1650 over 550, which is 3.0. The best ratio is 3.1111, for the first option. The total cost is 450 plus 650 plus 380 plus 850 plus 550, which is 2880, and the total benefit is 1400 plus 1800 plus 1180 plus 2300 plus 1650, which is 8330. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: score = benefit / cost for each option; best = the option with the largest score; total cost = sum of costs; total benefit = sum of benefits. The equation states the choice check the demo performs: it scores every option by benefit divided by cost, picks the largest score as the best option, and totals the costs and benefits.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of combining prediction with optimization: the prediction says when the failure will come, and the optimization says when to act, balancing the cost of acting early against the cost of failing late. The design follows the public evidence that the combination is worth more than either part alone, and it makes the outcome the measure. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A prescriptive choice compares options by what each one returns for what it costs; the best option is the one with the largest benefit per unit of cost.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
options = [('maintenance 1', 450, 1400), ('maintenance 2', 650, 1800), ('maintenance 3', 380, 1180), ('maintenance 4', 850, 2300), ('maintenance 5', 550, 1650)]
def compute():
    scored = [(b / c, name) for name, c, b in options]
    best_ratio_raw, best = max(scored)
    best_ratio = round(best_ratio_raw, 4)
    total_cost = sum(c for _, c, _ in options)
    total_benefit = sum(b for _, _, b in options)
    out = {"best": best, "best_ratio": best_ratio, "total_cost": total_cost, "total_benefit": total_benefit}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class ProgJAHPRAIS000019:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        options = [('maintenance 1', 450, 1400), ('maintenance 2', 650, 1800), ('maintenance 3', 380, 1180), ('maintenance 4', 850, 2300), ('maintenance 5', 550, 1650)]
        def compute():
            scored = [(b / c, name) for name, c, b in options]
            best_ratio_raw, best = max(scored)
            best_ratio = round(best_ratio_raw, 4)
            total_cost = sum(c for _, c, _ in options)
            total_benefit = sum(b for _, _, b in options)
            out = {"best": best, "best_ratio": best_ratio, "total_cost": total_cost, "total_benefit": total_benefit}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHPRAIS000019().run())

Signature Game Table: A Design for Strategy Against an Opponent JAH-PRAIS-000020 · 20

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Game Table is a Signature-made design for prescribing a strategy against an opponent, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design lays out the players, their options, and their payoffs as a table, finds the equilibria where no player can do better by changing alone, and prescribes the strategy with the equilibrium and the reasoning shown. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of game theory from Nash in 1950 and the spectrum auctions of the 1990s. The design takes the table idea, that a game is clearest when its payoffs are laid out, and adds the equilibrium count: the table reports how many equilibria it has, so that the prescription can say which one is intended. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Game Table is a Signature-made design for prescribing a strategy against an opponent, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design lays out the players, their options, and their payoffs as a table, finds the equilibria where no player can do better by changing alone, and prescribes the strategy with the equilibrium and the reasoning shown. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Game Table, a Signature-made design-study for game-theoretic prescription, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the players, their options, and their payoffs. Step 2: lay the game out as a table, and find the equilibria. Step 3: choose among the equilibria, by payoff, by fairness, or by the mechanism's design. Step 4: prescribe the strategy, and report the equilibrium and the reasoning. The demo runs a small worked example of the counting step with illustrative design values: it takes five games with equilibria found of 1, 2, 1, 3, and 2, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"total_equilibria_design": 9, "mean_equilibria_design": 1.8, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the table lays out, finds, chooses, and prescribes, and the worked example shows the counting arithmetic. The five games yield 1, 2, 1, 3, and 2 equilibria, for a total of 9, a mean of 1.8, a smallest of 1, and a largest of 3. The result that matters is the design judgment: a table that counts its equilibria can say which one is intended, and one that does not cannot, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. When It Applies

The Signature Game Table design applies as a study for anyone prescribing a strategy: it shows how to lay out, find, choose, and prescribe. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the players are not rational, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The table is a design, not a system, so it has never prescribed a real strategy, and its counts are illustrative. The design assumes the players are rational and the payoffs can be stated, which is not always true, and a prescription that assumes rationality will fail against an irrational opponent. The design is a study of how a game should be laid out and prescribed, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a table exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of a ledger. The five games yield 1, 2, 1, 3, and 2 equilibria, which sum to 9, and 9 divided by 5 is 1.8. The smallest game yields 1 equilibrium and the largest yields 3. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with, and a design that cannot be checked is only a picture. Formal statement: total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts. The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of strategic reasoning: a player's best action depends on what the others do, so the solution must be a fixed point, a set of strategies that are best responses to each other. The design follows the public evidence that the rules of the game decide the outcome as much as the players do, and it makes the rules part of the table. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('game 1', 1), ('game 2', 2), ('game 3', 1), ('game 4', 3), ('game 5', 2)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
