import hashlib
class ProgJAHPRAIS000020:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        ledger = [('game 1', 1), ('game 2', 2), ('game 3', 1), ('game 4', 3), ('game 5', 2)]
        def compute():
            counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
            total = round(sum(counts), 4)
            mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
            smallest = min(counts)
            largest = max(counts)
            n = len(counts)
            out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHPRAIS000020().run())

Signature Robust Optimizer: A Design for the Worst Case JAH-PRAIS-000021 · 21

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Robust Optimizer is a Signature-made design for prescribing the action that does best in the worst case, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design states the uncertainty set, solves for the action that maximizes the objective in the worst case within the set, and reports the price of robustness, the loss against the action that would be best if the parameter were known. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of robust optimization from Ben-Tal and Nemirovski in the 1990s. The design takes the worst-case idea, and adds the price report: the optimizer always states what the protection costs, so the decision maker can judge whether it is worth it. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Robust Optimizer is a Signature-made design for prescribing the action that does best in the worst case, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design states the uncertainty set, solves for the action that maximizes the objective in the worst case within the set, and reports the price of robustness, the loss against the action that would be best if the parameter were known. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Robust Optimizer, a Signature-made design-study for optimization under uncertainty, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the decision, the objective, and the uncertainty set. Step 2: solve for the action that maximizes the objective in the worst case within the set. Step 3: compute the price of robustness, the loss against the action that would be best if the parameter were known. Step 4: report the robust action, the worst case, and the price, and let the decision maker judge. The demo runs a small worked example of the choice step with illustrative design values: it takes five robust options with costs of 75, 95, 60, 115, and 85 and benefits of 190, 230, 165, 270, and 210, and reports the best option by benefit per cost, the total cost, and the total benefit. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"best_ratio_design": 2.75, "total_cost_design": 430, "total_benefit_design": 1065, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the optimizer states the set, solves the worst case, and reports the price, and the worked example shows the choice arithmetic. The benefit-per-cost ratios are 2.5333, 2.4211, 2.75, 2.3478, and 2.4706, so the best option is the third, with a ratio of 2.75, and the totals are a cost of 430 and a benefit of 1065. The result that matters is the design judgment: an optimizer that reports its price can be judged, and one that hides it cannot, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: best: robust 3, best_ratio: 2.75, total_benefit: 1065, total_cost: 430.

When It Applies

The Signature Robust Optimizer design applies as a study for anyone deciding under uncertainty: it shows how to state the set, solve the worst case, and report the price. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the uncertainty cannot be bounded, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The optimizer is a design, not a system, so it has never optimized a real decision, and its values are illustrative. The design assumes the uncertainty can be bounded, which is not always true, and a robust prescription can be so cautious that it forgoes most of the value. The design is a study of how a robust optimizer should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such an optimizer exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of ratios. The five options have benefit-per-cost ratios of 190 over 75, which is 2.5333; 230 over 95, which is 2.4211; 165 over 60, which is 2.75; 270 over 115, which is 2.3478; and 210 over 85, which is 2.4706. The best ratio is 2.75, for the third option. The total cost is 75 plus 95 plus 60 plus 115 plus 85, which is 430, and the total benefit is 190 plus 230 plus 165 plus 270 plus 210, which is 1065. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: score = benefit / cost for each option; best = the option with the largest score; total cost = sum of costs; total benefit = sum of benefits. The equation states the choice check the demo performs: it scores every option by benefit divided by cost, picks the largest score as the best option, and totals the costs and benefits.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of the worst case: an action that is best on average can be disastrous in the worst case, and a decision maker who cannot afford the disaster should optimize the worst case instead. The design follows the public evidence that the uncertainty set is the key judgment, too wide and the prescription is useless, too narrow and it protects against nothing, and it makes the set part of the report. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A prescriptive choice compares options by what each one returns for what it costs; the best option is the one with the largest benefit per unit of cost.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
options = [('robust 1', 75, 190), ('robust 2', 95, 230), ('robust 3', 60, 165), ('robust 4', 115, 270), ('robust 5', 85, 210)]
def compute():
    scored = [(b / c, name) for name, c, b in options]
    best_ratio_raw, best = max(scored)
    best_ratio = round(best_ratio_raw, 4)
    total_cost = sum(c for _, c, _ in options)
    total_benefit = sum(b for _, _, b in options)
    out = {"best": best, "best_ratio": best_ratio, "total_cost": total_cost, "total_benefit": total_benefit}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ProgJAHPRAIS000021:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        options = [('robust 1', 75, 190), ('robust 2', 95, 230), ('robust 3', 60, 165), ('robust 4', 115, 270), ('robust 5', 85, 210)]
        def compute():
            scored = [(b / c, name) for name, c, b in options]
            best_ratio_raw, best = max(scored)
            best_ratio = round(best_ratio_raw, 4)
            total_cost = sum(c for _, c, _ in options)
            total_benefit = sum(b for _, _, b in options)
            out = {"best": best, "best_ratio": best_ratio, "total_cost": total_cost, "total_benefit": total_benefit}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}

Signature Farm Planner: A Design for Prescribing for the Field JAH-PRAIS-000022 · 22

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Farm Planner is a Signature-made design for prescribing how much seed, fertilizer, and water each part of a field needs, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design maps the field, builds a prescription map from the soil data and the yield history, and applies it with variable-rate machinery, measuring the yield and the input use against the uniform baseline. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of precision agriculture from yield mapping in the 1990s through GPS-guided application in the 2000s. The design takes the spatial-variation idea, that a field is not uniform, and adds the baseline rule: the saving is always measured against the uniform rate, so that the value of the prescription can be counted. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Farm Planner is a Signature-made design for prescribing how much seed, fertilizer, and water each part of a field needs, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design maps the field, builds a prescription map from the soil data and the yield history, and applies it with variable-rate machinery, measuring the yield and the input use against the uniform baseline. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Farm Planner, a Signature-made design-study for precision agriculture, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: map the field, with soil data, yield history, and sensor readings. Step 2: build a prescription map, stating the rate for each part of the field. Step 3: apply the map with variable-rate machinery, and record what was applied where. Step 4: measure the yield and the input use against the uniform baseline, and update the map. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step with illustrative design values: it takes five field zones with prescribed fertilizer rates of 115, 85, 145, 75, and 105 kilograms per hectare against applied rates of 113, 87, 143, 77, and 103, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mae_design": 2.0, "rmse_design": 2.0, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the planner maps, prescribes, applies, and measures against the baseline, and the worked example shows the error arithmetic. The gaps are 2, 2, 2, 2, and 2, giving a mean absolute error of 2.0 and a root mean square error of 2.0, with a largest gap of 2. The result that matters is the design judgment: a planner that measures against the baseline can prove its value, and one that does not cannot, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 2.0, max_err: 2, n: 5, rmse: 2.0.

When It Applies

The Signature Farm Planner design applies as a study for anyone building a precision-agriculture system: it shows how to map, prescribe, apply, and measure against the baseline. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the field is uniform, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The planner is a design, not a system, so it has never prescribed a real field, and its values are illustrative. The design assumes the mapping is accurate, which is not always true, and a map built on thin data will prescribe noise. The design is a study of how a farm planner should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a planner exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 2, 2, 2, 2, and 2, which sum to 10, so the mean absolute error is 10 divided by 5, which is 2.0. The squared gaps are 4, 4, 4, 4, and 4, which sum to 20, so the mean squared error is 4.0 and the root mean square error is the square root of 4.0, which is 2.0. The largest single gap is 2. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of spatial variation: a field's soil varies from place to place, and the crop's needs vary with it, so a uniform rate is wrong everywhere. The design follows the public evidence that variable-rate application saves inputs where the soil is rich and feeds the crop where it is poor, and it makes the baseline comparison the measure. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(115, 113), (85, 87), (145, 143), (75, 77), (105, 103)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ProgJAHPRAIS000022:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(115, 113), (85, 87), (145, 143), (75, 77), (105, 103)]
        def compute():
            errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
            mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
            rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
            max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
            n = len(pairs)
            out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHPRAIS000022().run())

Signature Factory Optimizer: A Design for Process Settings JAH-PRAIS-000023 · 23

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Factory Optimizer is a Signature-made design for prescribing the settings of a manufacturing process, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design measures the process, charts it, designs experiments to find the settings that improve the quality, and prescribes them, monitoring the new settings and re-prescribing as the process drifts. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of statistical process control from Shewhart in the 1920s and design of experiments from Fisher in the 1920s and 1930s. The design takes the control-chart idea, that common-cause variation stays inside the limits and special-cause variation falls outside them, and makes the chart part of the optimizer, so that the prescription is never made from a process that is out of control. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Factory Optimizer is a Signature-made design for prescribing the settings of a manufacturing process, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design measures the process, charts it, designs experiments to find the settings that improve the quality, and prescribes them, monitoring the new settings and re-prescribing as the process drifts. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Factory Optimizer, a Signature-made design-study for manufacturing process optimization, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: measure the process, with its settings and its quality outcomes. Step 2: chart the measurements, and watch for drift beyond the control limits. Step 3: design experiments to find the settings that improve the quality, and prescribe them. Step 4: monitor the new settings, and re-prescribe as the process drifts. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step with illustrative design values: it takes five process settings with predicted quality scores of 0.9, 0.86, 0.93, 0.83, and 0.88 against measured scores of 0.88, 0.88, 0.91, 0.85, and 0.87, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mae_design": 0.018, "rmse_design": 0.0184, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the optimizer measures, charts, experiments, and re-prescribes, and the worked example shows the error arithmetic. The gaps are 0.02, 0.02, 0.02, 0.02, and 0.01, giving a mean absolute error of 0.018 and a root mean square error of 0.0184, with a largest gap of 0.02. The result that matters is the design judgment: an optimizer that checks the control chart before prescribing is honest, and one that prescribes from an out-of-control process is not, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 0.018, max_err: 0.02, n: 5, rmse: 0.0184.

When It Applies

The Signature Factory Optimizer design applies as a study for anyone optimizing a manufacturing process: it shows how to measure, chart, experiment, and re-prescribe. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the process is not measured, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The optimizer is a design, not a system, so it has never optimized a real process, and its scores are illustrative. The design assumes the measurements are reliable, which is not always true, and the settings that are optimal today may not be optimal tomorrow. The design is a study of how a factory optimizer should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such an optimizer exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 0.02, 0.02, 0.02, 0.02, and 0.01, which sum to 0.09, so the mean absolute error is 0.09 divided by 5, which is 0.018. The squared gaps are 0.0004, 0.0004, 0.0004, 0.0004, and 0.0001, which sum to 0.0017, so the mean squared error is 0.00034 and the root mean square error is the square root of 0.00034, which is 0.0184. The largest single gap is 0.02. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of variation: every process varies, and the variation has causes, some common and some special. The design follows the public evidence that the control chart separates them, and that the design of experiments finds the settings that matter with far fewer runs than varying one at a time. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.9, 0.88), (0.86, 0.88), (0.93, 0.91), (0.83, 0.85), (0.88, 0.87)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature Prescription Workbench: A Design for the Whole Prescriptive Craft JAH-PRAIS-000024 · 24

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Prescription Workbench is a Signature-made design for the whole craft of prescription, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design gathers the parts the other designs in this edition describe separately, the solver, the simulator, the optimizer, the oversight console, and the auditor, into one workbench, so that a prescriber can move from modeling to prescription to oversight without changing tools. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of prescriptive analytics from operations research in the 1940s through the optimization and simulation methods of the present. The design takes the lesson of that history, that prescription is a cycle from modeling through solving, testing, and oversight, and makes the cycle the architecture: one workbench, five stations, and a ledger that follows every prescription from the model it was solved from to the audit where it is checked. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Prescription Workbench is a Signature-made design for the whole craft of prescription, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design gathers the parts the other designs in this edition describe separately, the solver, the simulator, the optimizer, the oversight console, and the auditor, into one workbench, so that a prescriber can move from modeling to prescription to oversight without changing tools. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 4: oversee the prescription through the console, and audit it on a schedule. The demo runs a small worked example of the counting step with illustrative design values: it takes five workbench stations with prescriptions handled of 105, 80, 125, 95, and 115, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The result that matters is the design judgment: a craft needs one workbench for the whole cycle, because a cycle split across tools loses the ledger that ties a prescription to its model and its audit, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. Formal equation

total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts

Formal equation, in plain words

The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Formal status

stated, not machine-checked

Scientific equation

A ledger of counts is summarised by its total, its mean, and its extremes; those four numbers are the checkable summary of the record's figures.

Proof sketch

The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Proof status

The arithmetic is machine-checked by the demo; the scientific claim is stated, not machine-checked.

Results reported

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the workbench covers modeling, rehearsal, optimization, oversight, and audit, and the worked example shows the counting arithmetic. The five stations handle 105, 80, 125, 95, and 115 prescriptions, for a total of 520, a mean of 104.0, a smallest of 80, and a largest of 125. The result that matters is the design judgment: a craft needs one workbench for the whole cycle, because a cycle split across tools loses the ledger that ties a prescription to its model and its audit, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 125, mean: 104.0, n: 5, smallest: 80, total: 520.

Skeptical analysis

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The workbench is a design, not a system, so it has never prescribed a real action, and its counts are illustrative. The design assumes the whole cycle can be served by one tool, which is a bet, and it cannot solve the model error, the ethics, or the accountability, it can only make them visible. The design is a study of how the craft should be tooled, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a workbench exists or works.

Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('modeling', 105), ('rehearsal', 80), ('optimization', 125), ('oversight', 95), ('audit', 115)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class ProgJAHPRAIS000024:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        ledger = [('modeling', 105), ('rehearsal', 80), ('optimization', 125), ('oversight', 95), ('audit', 115)]
        def compute():
            counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
            total = round(sum(counts), 4)
            mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
            smallest = min(counts)
            largest = max(counts)
            n = len(counts)
            out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHPRAIS000024().run())
